Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    1 
It is my pleasure to present to you this compilation of statistics that describe the vast 
and diverse California agriculture economy for the 2023 crop year. California’s 
farms and ranches received $59.4 billion in cash receipts for their output.  This 
value represents a 1.4 percent increase over the previous year and is a new 
record for California!  California agricultural exports were valued at $22.4 billion 
for the same period.  
Tempering all that is the recognition that increasing production costs, skilled labor 
challenges, water security issues, and invasive pest and animal disease threats 
all require continued work in climate adaptation and resiliency.  This work is critical 
to ensuring our ability to withstand extreme weather events and achieve technological 
innovation on a grand scale!
Once again, California is the top agricultural state in the country, accounting for nearly two-thirds of the nation’s 
production of vegetables, fruits and nuts during 2023.  The strength of California’s agriculture sector rests on 
the tremendous diversity of our crops, our resilient farming families, our optimal growing environment, and the 
modern miracle of our water infrastructure.  
Maintaining and growing our state position as a net supplier of food and fiber to the local, national, and global 
marketplaces requires not just leadership and innovation, but ongoing investments in farming operations, and 
assurance that food safety, public health, and worker safety are top priorities, alongside our commitment as 
stewards of our working lands and the environment.  These are all outcomes that we are proud of in this state and 
enhance what it means to be CA GROWN! 
      
Thank you for your interest in this report and in California agriculture. The California Agricultural Statistics Review 
is the result of a joint effort between the U.S. Department of Agriculture’s National Agricultural Statistics Service – 
California Field Office, and the California Department of Food and Agriculture. On behalf of the staff who collect, 
analyze and publish this important data, it is an honor to share this report with you. 
Yours truly, 
Karen Ross, Secretary 
California Department of Food and Agriculture
Foreword


=== PDF PAGE 4 ===
Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    2 
The sales value generated by California agriculture 
increased by 1.4 percent between the 2022 and 
2023 crop years. The state’s 62,900 farms and 
ranches received $59.4 billion for their output, up 
from $58.6 billion received in 2022. California’s 
agricultural revenue was led by the dairy industry 
followed by grapes, miscellaneous crops (which 
includes nursery and greenhouse crops), and cattle 
and calves.
The dairy industry, California’s leading commodity 
in cash receipts, generated $8.1 billion for milk 
production in 2023, down 21.8 percent from 2022.  
Milk production decreased by 2.1 percent and 
milk prices received by producers decreased from 
$24.90 per hundredweight of milk sold in 2022 to 
$19.90 per hundredweight in 2023. As the leading 
dairy producing state in the country, California 
produced 17.7 percent of the nation’s supply in 
2023.
Grape production generated $6.5 billion in cash 
receipts in 2023, up 16.5 percent from 2022. 
Production increased by 0.4 percent from 2022, and 
prices received by growers increased from $1,010 
per ton of grapes in 2022 to $1,170 per ton in 2023.
Total cash receipts generated from cattle and calves 
in 2023 was $4.8 billion, up 31.3 percent from 2022.  
Cattle and calves accounted for 30.2 percent of the 
state’s total livestock receipts for 2023.
California remained the leading state in cash 
farm receipts in 2023 with combined commodities 
representing 11.3 percent of the U.S. total. 
California’s leading crops remained fruits, nuts and 
vegetables.
California organic sales have increased by 21.7 
percent from $9.7 billion in 2019 to $11.8 billion in 
2023. Organic production site acreage in the state 
has decreased by 13.0 percent from 2,051,249.17 
acres in 2019 to 1,784,011.01 acres in 2023. 
California accounts for 32 percent of organic sales in 
the U.S.

Farm Facts 
In 2023, 62,900 farms operated in California, down 
0.3 percent from 2022. Thirty-three percent of 
California farms generated commodity sales over 
$100,000, exceeding the national average of 21.0 
percent. The amount of land devoted to farming and 
ranching in California was 23.8 million acres in 2023, 
down from 24.2 million acres in 2022.  The average 
farm size was 378 acres in 2023, down from 384 
acres in 2022 and below the 2023 national average 
farm size of 464 acres.
Agricultural Statistical Overview

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    3 
Land Values/Cash Rents
The average value per acre of California farm 
real estate in 2023 was $13,100, up 4.0 percent 
from the 2022 value of $12,600 per acre. During 
the year, the value of irrigated cropland increased 
3.7 percent to $19,700 per acre, and the value 
of non-irrigated cropland increased 6.8 percent 
to $7,400 per acre. The average value of all 
cropland increased 4.0 percent, compared to the 
previous year, to $16,820 per acre. The value 
of pastureland increased 3.5 percent to $3,860 
per acre.  The rental rate of irrigated cropland 
increased to $486 per acre in 2023, up from $465 
per acre in 2022. Pastureland rental rates were $13 
per acre in 2023, unchanged from 2022.  
Top Commodities
California’s top 20 crops and livestock commodities 
accounted for $50.9 billion in value in 2023. Eleven 
commodities exceeded $1 billion in value in 2023. 
The cash receipts of 11 of the top 20 commodities 
increased in value in 2023, compared to 2022. Of 
the top 20 commodities, pistachios, walnuts, carrots, 
tomatoes, and cattle and calves showed the largest 
growth in cash receipts during the year.
Weather Highlights for 2023
The year began wet as a large upper trough pulled 
in tropical moisture over the last days of December 
2022 into New Year’s Day. Heavy precipitation 
and widespread flooding across much of the state 
closed some principal roads with flooding, debris 
flows, and downed trees. Eighteen to 30 inches 
of snow fell in the upper elevations of the Sierra 
Nevada. Dense morning fog formed in the San 
Joaquin Valley after the storm moved eastward. 
This was quickly followed by another atmospheric 
river, bringing heavy precipitation and high winds. 
Already saturated ground led to considerable 
nuisance flooding, some mudflows, and slides in 
the Sierra foothills. The warm storm pushed snow 
levels up above 7,000 feet. Winds diminished and 
skies cleared behind the storm, setting up conditions 
for the typical winter feature of dense morning 
fog. A cool weak low-pressure system moved 
over northern California with light precipitation 
and snow down to 5,000 feet in Yosemite. The 
second week of January saw a strong low-pressure 
system bearing significant tropical moisture move 
through central California, dropping 3 to 6 inches 
of rain in the Sierra Nevada. The snow level was 
around 7,500 feet, resulting in heavy runoff of 
snow accumulations from earlier colder storms. 
Flooding impacted state highways and prompted 
local evacuations. On January 14, a slow moving 
strong low-pressure system impacted the state with 
heavy precipitation primarily in the north. Snow 
levels dropped with this cold front to 4,500 feet, and 
up to 3 feet of new snow fell at higher elevations. 
Again, widespread flooding impacted many areas 
already saturated by recent storms. On January 20, 
a large dry upper high-pressure ridge built over the 
state, bringing mainly clear skies and light winds. 
As a result, dense fog blanketed the central valley 
floor in the mornings as the high-pressure system 
lingered until the final days of the month. A cold 
upper low-pressure system dropped southward 
over northern California on January 29 and then 
dropped further southward off the central and 
southern California coast, providing more moisture 
to the state. The snow level lowered to 2,000 feet, 
allowing more snow accumulation. The month 
ended with a cold air mass settling over the state, 
promoting some localized frost and valley morning 
fog. Statewide January precipitation was over 204 
percent of the 20-year norm. The statewide average 
minimum temperature was the seventh warmest 
since records commenced in 1895. January began 
with just over 71 percent of the state experiencing 
severe drought conditions and ended with only 32.6 
percent classified as suffering severe drought. The 
statewide snowpack water equivalence on January 
3 was 185 percent of normal after the cold wet 
storms of December. By the end of January, this 
had increased to only 214 percent of normal, due 
in part to snowmelt from several warm storms. The 
month’s storms helped fill reservoirs. Lake Oroville’s 
water level increased from 55 percent of the historic

Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    4 
average to 112 percent of average, and Lake Shasta 
rose from 57 percent of the historic average to 87 
percent of average over the month.
February started cold and dry. Some low locations 
in the San Joaquin Valley reported frost on the first 
day of the month. A strong low-pressure system 
moved east over northern California on February 5 
and pushed down across the central San Joaquin 
Valley. This quick moving system brought rain 
to the valleys and snow down to 4,000 feet. The 
cold storm dropped snow on the Tejon Pass in the 
Tehachapi Mountains. An upper-level ridge cleared 
out the skies and allowed morning fog to form in the 
valleys for much of the following week. A cold upper 
trough dropped from the Pacific Northwest into the 
Great Basin on February 14. The moisture deficient 
system left only traces of precipitation in the Sierra 
Nevada and Tehachapi Mountains. The cold dry 
air mass and light winds nudged temperatures 
down enough to have some locations in the central 
valley’s report frost. During the last week of the 
month, a large cold upper-level low-pressure system 
dropped slowly off the coast, bringing up to 3 inches 
of precipitation and snow levels down to 2,000 feet 
in the Sierra Nevada. Some stations reported up to 
4 feet of new snow. The month ended with another 
cold trough swinging across the state, resulting in 
widespread rain and snow down to 3,000 feet. The 
statewide mean temperature for February was down 
4.2 °F from the norm. By the end of the month, the 
U.S. Drought Monitor classified only 19 percent of 
the state under severe drought conditions. Lake 
Oroville’s level was 116 percent of average by the 
end of February. The snowpack water equivalent for 
the state was at 198 percent of normal on March 3, 
the third survey of the water year.
The cold wet conditions continued into March, with 
precipitation across much of the state and snow 
in the Tehachapi Mountains. Snow levels dropped 
to 2,000 feet in the Southern Sierra Nevada and 
over 2 feet of new snow was recorded near Kings 
Canyon. As the second week of the month began, 
another atmospheric river pulling in warm tropical 
moisture produced up to 8 inches of precipitation 
in the Sierras. Melting snow and already saturated 
soils led to catastrophic flooding across the foothills 
and valleys. This storm was quickly followed by 
another, though bearing less moisture. The cold 
air mass behind this wave triggered thunderstorms 
and localized flooding. A few days later, a warm 
wet upper-level trough dropped more rain across 
the state and elevated snow levels to above 6,000 
feet. Several rivers reached flood stage due to the 
excessive snow runoff. The month ended with a 
strong low-pressure system bringing more snow 
to the Sierras, with up to 2 feet reported at some 
stations. This was the fifth wettest March in the prior 
50 years. The statewide average mean temperature 
was the third coldest since 1895. Despite the snow 
melt, the statewide average snow water equivalent 
on April 3 was 250 percent of normal. Lake Shasta 
was 104 percent of the historic average by the end 
of March, while Lake Oroville was at 116 percent 
of average. On April 4, the US Drought Monitor 
estimated only 0.82 percent of the state was still 
experiencing severe drought conditions.
An upper-level trough dropped over the Great Basin 
during the first week of April. This drove strong 
winds across the mountains and valleys, several 
inches of snow above 6,000 feet in the Sierras, and 
a trace of snow near Tejon Pass in the Tehachapi 
Mountains. During the second week of April, a dry 
upper-level trough generated high winds. This was 
quickly followed by a moisture laden system that 
brought light precipitation to the northern edge of 
the state. As April ended, a low-pressure system 
settled over the Great Basin, generating a strong 
pressure gradient that produced high winds across 
the mountains and Mojave Desert. Throughout the 
month, melting snow led to high river flows, damage 
to some bridges, and localized flooding. The 
statewide average mean temperature for April was 
53.6 °F, only -0.6 °F below the monthly norm. The 
April statewide average precipitation was only 33.5 
percent of normal. The statewide average snowpack 
water equivalent was 279 percent of normal on May 
1. The water level at Lake Shasta ended the month 
at 115 percent of the historic average, while Lake 
Oroville was at 118 percent of average. On May 2, 
the US Drought Monitor estimated no part of the 
state was experiencing severe drought conditions. 
The last time no severe drought areas were seen in 
the state was March 10, 2020.
May started off wet as an upper-level low-pressure 
system slowly moved across the state. The low 
brought scattered showers to most of the state. After 
a few warm dry days, another system swung over 
the state, resulting in thunderstorms and localized 
heavy downpours. Midmonth, a strong upper ridge

Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    5 
developed and warmed the state. The San Joaquin 
Valley saw its first triple-digit temperatures as the 
ridge persisted. Gulf of Mexico moisture was drawn 
in over the Sierras, fueling scattered thunderstorms 
in the Sierra Nevada. The ridge weakened and 
temperatures moderated. The month ended with a 
wet Pacific Northwest storm skirting the northern 
regions of the state, bringing some light showers. 
The statewide mean average temperature was only 
0.3 °F above the norm. The statewide precipitation 
was down nearly 10 percent from average. The 
winter’s snow load and cool spring put the statewide 
average snowpack water equivalent at 338 percent 
of normal on June 1. Many of the state’s reservoirs 
were filled well above historic averages. At the 
end of May, Lake Shasta was 117 percent of its 
historic norm, and Lake Oroville was 122 percent 
of its historic norm. Water releases from the filling 
reservoirs kept many rivers near record flows for 
May. 
June began dry with seasonal temperatures near 
normal. A low-pressure system approached the 
southern coast near the end of the first week of 
June, resulting in a few thunderstorms and localized 
heavy rainfall. Golf ball-sized hail was reported 
in Kern County. The following week, another low 
pushed over central California, again bringing 
thunderstorms and localized heavy downpours. This 
system brought light showers to most of the state. 
The third week of June saw a large upper-level 
trough move over the state, which brought much 
cooler temperatures than normal. The month ended 
with an area of high-pressure building over the 
state, resulting in the first heat wave of the season. 
The statewide mean average temperature was the 
fourth coolest in 25 years. The cool May contributed 
to the persistence of the snowpack. The June 1 
snow water equivalent statewide was 388 percent 
of normal. The reservoir water levels remained high 
with the ongoing snowmelt and water managers

California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    6 
careful regulation of releases to mitigate flooding. 
Lake Shasta was at 117 percent of its historic level 
on May 31and Lake Oroville was 123 percent of its 
historic norm.
The year’s first heat wave continued into July as 
the high-pressure region remained centered over 
the state. After the Independence Day holiday, 
temperatures cooled by 4 °F to 5 °F as the high 
moved eastward. At the end of the second week 
of July, a high-pressure system built over the 
desert southwest. This again elevated the state’s 
temperatures well above seasonal norms. The heat 
wave was mitigated as the high weakened over 
the last days of the month. The statewide average 
mean temperature for July was the fifth hottest in 
the state’s recorded history. Snowmelt and reservoir 
releases kept the river flows elevated. The Lake 
Shasta water level at the end of July was 125 
percent of the historic norm, while Lake Oroville was 
138 percent of the historic norm.
August started mostly dry with near normal 
temperatures. Tropical moisture, pulled up from 
the Gulf of Mexico, brought up to 0.5 inch of rain 
in the Mojave Desert. An upper-level low-pressure 
system, the remnant moisture of the former Tropical 
Storm Eugene, was situated off the coast on August 
12. This moisture fueled scattered thunderstorms 
over the Sierra Nevada. A large area of high-
pressure shifted westward from the Four Corners 
region to central California, resulting in a period of 
very high temperatures midmonth. Some stations 
in the Sacramento and San Joaquin Valleys 
reported temperatures 7 °F to 9 °F above normal. 
Thunderstorms and scattered precipitation fell 
across the Sierra Nevada. As the high weakened, a 
cooling trend began on August 18. Tropical Storm 
Hilary crossed the US-Mexico border into California 
on August 20, becoming the first tropical storm in 
the state since Nora in 1997. The strong surge of 
moisture associated with Hilary caused moderate to 
heavy rainfall across the south state. As the storm 
moved north, it broke precipitation records for the 
typically dry month. South of Fresno, there were 
reports of scattered flash flooding, debris flows, 
funnel clouds, and gusty winds downing trees. 
Behind the tropical storm, dry and warm conditions 
returned for the remainder of the month. The 
southern half of the state accumulated the bulk of 
the month’s precipitation. The statewide average 
precipitation was 1.47 inches, or over 816 percent of 
normal. By the end of the month, Lake Shasta stood 
at 129 percent of the historic level, and Lake Oroville 
was 139 percent of its historic norm. Statewide, the 
average minimum daily temperature was 62.2 °F,

Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    7 
making it the sixth warmest average August low 
since 1895. 
The month of September began cool with some 
light showers along the Sierra Nevada as tropical 
moisture was pulled up along the mountain range. 
Temperatures across most of the state warmed up 
to or just above seasonal norms until a cold front 
dropped over the state in the third week of the 
month. Thunderstorms and scattered rain impacted 
the Sierras and the northern half of the state. As 
September ended, a strong upper low-pressure 
center moved over the north state. This brought 
rain to the north and the season’s first snowstorm. 
Several stations reported 2 to 5 inches of snow 
above 8,000 feet. The statewide average daily high 
temperature was 5.5 °F below normal, making it 
the twelfth coolest September since 1895. Though 
typically a dry month, September of 2023 was 
nearly twice as wet as normal, with a statewide 
precipitation of 0.6 inches. The reservoirs remained 
fuller than normal. Lake Shasta ended the month of 
September at 131 percent of its historic level. Lake 
Oroville was 136 percent of its historic normal.
Traces of precipitation covered much of the state 
as October began and the late September storm 
moved eastward. Dry and warm conditions prevailed 
as a high-pressure ridge developed over the state. 
Much of the state saw temperatures 3 °F to 7 °F 
above the seasonal norm. An upper trough dropped 
southeast of the Gulf of Alaska to over the Great 
Basin during the second week of October. The 
resulting strong gradient produced high winds 
across the Sierra Nevada and Mojave Desert. 
Some precipitation fell over the North Coast region 
of the state as the trough swung across the Pacific 
Northwest. Temperatures dropped to more typical 
levels. Another brief wave of hot weather followed 
with unusually high temperatures across the state. 
The Blue Canyon station, at 6,000 feet in the 
Sierra Nevada Mountains, recorded temperatures 
11 °F above the norm. The edge of a wet storm 
over the Pacific Northwest brushed the north state 
and coastal regions with light precipitation. The 
month ended dry and cool. The statewide monthly 
precipitation total was only 0.52 inches, or 42 
percent of normal. The statewide average minimum 
temperature was 2.1 °F below historic norms, 
making it the third coolest low for October since 
1895.
The first two days of November were warm and 
dry. Then, the southern edge of a Pacific Northwest 
storm brought some traces of precipitation to the 
north state. This was followed by a series of weak 
fronts moving over the state, dropping light rain 
across the state from Kern County northward 
and some snow in the Sierra above 8,000 feet. 
Midmonth, a frontal system moved over central 
California, pulling in moisture from an offshore 
upper low. This resulted in widespread, though 
light, precipitation for most of the state. The warm 
system pushed the snow level to above 10,000 feet. 
After the front moved east, clear skies and remnant 
moisture promoted the season’s first widespread fog 
formation in the San Joaquin Valley. An upper-level 
low-pressure system that had stalled off the coast 
moved inland, resulting in precipitation covering the 
state north of Monterey. Thunderstorms associated 
with the storm led to localized heavy downpours. 
The trough moved east out of the state, and a large 
upper ridge developed over the state. This kept the 
skies clear and winds light, allowing dense morning 
fog to form in parts of the San Joaquin Valley for 
several days. A dry cold trough then pushed through 
the region. The month ended with another series 
of weak fronts, with only traces of rain reported 
along the coastal range and westside of the central 
valleys. The statewide average precipitation for 
the month was 40 percent below the November 
norm. The statewide monthly average daily low 
temperature was warmer than normal.
December opened with an atmospheric river over 
the Pacific Northwest. The southern edge of this wet 
system doused the north state with rain. Portions 
of Del Norte and Humboldt counties received 2 to 4 
inches of precipitation, while the Sacramento Valley 
only had light showers. During the second week, 
an Arctic air mass settled over the San Joaquin 
Valley. As a result, minimum temperatures ranged 
between 28 °F and 31 °F for many locations in the 
valley. The north coast received some precipitation 
as an upper-level low stalled over the Pacific. The 
low-pressure system moved over the state in mid-
December bringing widespread precipitation. The 
warm system raised the snow level to above 7,000 
feet. A dry upper ridge developed over the San 
Joaquin Valley bringing dense fog to regions of the 
valley over the Christmas holiday. The north state 
saw some rain as another Pacific Northwest storm 
brushed the state. As the year ended, an upper-
level low-pressure system moved over the state. 

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    8 
The system brought strong winds and widespread 
precipitation. The statewide December precipitation 
was 3.41 inches, or 79.7 percent of normal. The 
statewide average mean temperature was 4.2 °F 
above the norm and the fourth warmest December 
since 1895. Lake Shasta’s water level at the end of 
the month was 110 percent of the historic average. 
This was down from the end of November due to 
releases and limited runoff from the month’s storms. 
The January 2, 2024 snow survey revealed that the 
statewide snowpack was only 25 percent of normal 
for that date.

=== PDF PAGE 11 ===
Agricultural Statistical Overview
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    9 
Global Marketplace
California’s agricultural exports reached 
$22.4 billion in cash receipts for 2023, 
representing a decrease of 5.9 percent 
compared to 2022. In 2023, California 
accounted for 12.8 percent of total U.S. 
agricultural exports, up from 12.2 percent in 
2022.
Significantly, California is the nation’s sole 
exporter of many agricultural commodities, 
supplying 99 percent or more of the nation’s 
exported almonds, artichokes, dates, figs, 
garlic, kiwifruit, olives and olive oil, pistachios, 
prunes, raisins, table grapes, tomatoes for 
processing, and walnuts.
Commodities that experienced the largest 
year-over-year gains in export value include 
cherries, asparagus, olives and olive oil, 
tangerines and mandarins, and plums. 
Commodities that experienced the largest decreases in 
export value, compared to the prior year, include cottonseed 
and byproducts, prunes, hay, walnuts, and grapefruit.
California’s agricultural export values have grown by 4.0 
percent over the past 10 years.

County Statistical Data
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024     20 
In 2023, California counties reported a total value 
of agricultural production, excluding timber, of 
$67.0 billion, which represents a 1.2 percent 
increase from the prior year.  Seven counties 
reported a value of production exceeding $3.0 
billion each. Sixteen counties reported a value 
of production exceeding $1.0 billion each. Kern 
County was the leading county in the state, with an 
agricultural production value of $8.63 billion, which 
represents an increase of 12.0 percent from 2022. 
Fresno County was second in value of production 
for the year at $8.59 billion, which represents an 
increase of 6.2 percent from the prior year. Tulare 
County ranked third in value of production for the 
year, decreasing 8.7 percent from the previous 
year to $7.87 billion in 2023. Monterey County 
remained fourth in value of production at $4.35 
billion, which represents a decrease of 6.2 percent 
from 2022.
The total 2023 value of production reported by 
the counties, including timber, was $67.3 billion, 
which represents an increase of 1.3 percent from 
the prior year. During the year, Humboldt County 
recorded the highest timber value in the state at 
$102.7 million. Timber accounted for 40.5 percent 
of Humboldt County’s total agricultural value for 
the year.
Gross Value in Agricultural 
Commissioners’ Reports
The gross value of production presented in this 
section includes all farm production, whether 
sold through usual marketing channels or used 
on the farm where it is produced. This includes 
the value of pasture and range feed. Fresh fruit 
and vegetable crop values are based on the 
Free-On-Board (F.O.B.) packed price. Values 
are recorded for all products grown during the 
calendar year, regardless of when they are 
marketed. Consequently, the values summarized 
in this report are not comparable to the values 
estimated by USDA’s National Agricultural 
Statistics Service.
Some counties include timber as a part of their 
crop report, following the pattern set by the 
California County Agricultural Commissioners

County Statistical Data
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024      21 
and Sealers Association. Since it is not comparable 
to earlier county summaries, timber has not been 
made a part of the main tables of this section. 
County Agricultural Commissioners’ 
Reports
The information presented in this section is 
based on the annual crop reports compiled by the 
California County Agricultural Commissioners. 
These reports provide detailed annual data 
available on agricultural production by county.
Reports were received from 50 of the 58 counties 
for 2023. County Agricultural Commissioners’ 
Reports from the following counties were not 
available at the time of publication: Alameda, 
Contra Costa, Lassen, Mendocino, Siskiyou, 
Trinity, and Tuolumne. Data for Alameda, Contra 
Costa, Lassen, Mendocino, Siskiyou and 
Tuolumne counties were pulled forward from 2022, 
however, since data for Trinity County has not 
been available for the past three years, this county 
is not included in the tables presented in this 
section. Additionally, San Francisco County had no 
agricultural production in 2023 and therefore did 
not publish a County Agricultural Commissioners’ 
Report. San Francisco County is also not included 
in the tables presented in this section.
The methodology and data sources for the county 
reports varies by county. Data sources may 
include voluntary producer surveys, regulatory and 
inspection data, shipment data, and commodity 
board assessments. Copies of individual County 
Agricultural Commissioners’ Reports may be 
obtained directly from each County Agricultural 
Commissioner’s office or found at: cacasa.org/
crop-reports/
The level of detail of the data in the county reports 
varies by county. For example, one county may 
publish acreage and total value of production for all 
grapes, and another county may publish acreage, 
tonnage produced, and total value of production 
separately for wine grapes, table grapes, and 
raisin grapes. In this section, detailed data is 
published when it is available in the county’s 
report. Information presented in this section is also 
summed into commodity groups, such as “Grapes, 
All”, and commodity categories, such as “Fruit & 
Nuts” to assist with comparison between counties.  
Additionally, commodity names and groupings 
have been updated to allow for greater detail and 
comparability to the counties’ data.
Credit is due to the County Agricultural 
Commissioners and their staff members for 
preparing the annual county crop reports upon 
which this compilation is based.

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
Field Crops
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    29 
The total value of California field crop production 
for 2023 was $2.38 billion, down 18 percent from 
the prior year. Field crops with notable increases 
in value for 2023, as compared to the prior year, 
included rice, corn for grain, potatoes (excluding 
sweet), barley, sugar beets, and dry edible beans.  
Field crops with notable decreases in value for 
2023, as compared to the prior year, included 
safflower, cottonseed, cotton, haylage and 
greenchop, sunflower, and forage.
Of the field crops that increased in value for 2023, 
as compared to the previous year, rice experienced 
the highest increase in value, rising 62 percent.  
Corn for grain recorded the second highest 
increase in value at 55 percent above the previous 
year, and potatoes (excluding sweet) recorded the 
third highest increase in value at 47 percent above 
the previous year. 
The total value of all wheat in California decreased 
by 6 percent from 2022 to 2023. Winter wheat 
production in the state reached 192,000 tons in 
2023, which represents a 25 percent increase 
from the previous year. Conversely, Durum wheat 
production was recorded at 58,140 tons, which 
represents a 50 percent decrease from the prior 
year. A total of 338,000 acres of wheat were 
planted during 2023, of which 97,000 acres, or 29 
percent of the planted acres, were harvested for 
grain. The 2023 price of wheat averaged $303.67 
per ton, which represents a decrease of 13 
percent from the previous year.
There were 40,000 acres of barley planted in 
2023, unchanged from the previous year. Of 
the planted acres,19,000 acres were harvested 
for grain, which is the same as in 2022. Barley 
production was up 34 percent from the previous 
year to 34,200 tons in 2023, and the average price 
was down 33 percent from the previous year, to 
$220.83 per ton.  
California’s planted acreage of safflower was 
22,000 acres in 2023, which is a decrease of 
28,000 acres from the previous year. The 2023 
average price of safflower was $36.00 per cwt., up 
8 percent from the previous year. 
The total crop value for all hay decreased in 2023 
by 33 percent from the prior year. During the year, 
830,000 acres of hay were harvested in California, 
down 3 percent from the prior year. Additionally, for 
all hay in 2023, yield per acre was down 4 percent, 
production was down 8 percent, and the average 
price per ton was down 27 percent, as compared 
to 2022. For alfalfa hay, total production decreased 
by 10 percent from the previous year to 3.1 million 
tons, and total value decreased by 33 percent to 
$796 million. Production of hay (other than alfalfa) 
in California also decreased during the year, 
dropping from 1.18 million tons in 2022 to 1.16 
 Field Crops

California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    30 
million tons in 2023. The total crop value of $239 
million for hay (other than alfalfa) was 30 percent 
lower than the prior year. 
Of the field crops that decreased in value for 
2023, as compared to the previous year, safflower 
experienced the sharpest decline in value, 
dropping 55 percent. While the average price per 
ton of safflower was up from the previous year, 
acres harvested and yield per acre both decreased 
in 2023, down 55 percent and 7 percent, 
respectively, compared to 2022. 
Planted and harvested acres of upland cotton 
recorded record lows in 2023, with 13,000 acres 
planted and 12,800 acres harvested in California.  
Production was 54,000 bales, down 28 percent 
from 2022.  Planted and harvested acres of 
American-Pima cotton during the year were 85,000 
acres and 82,000 acres, respectively. Production 
was 230,000 bales, down 38 percent from the 
previous year. Total cotton production in the state 
was 284,000 bales in 2023, with an average yield 
of 1,438 pounds per acre, which represents an 11 
percent decline from the prior year. 
Following a significant decrease in acres of rice 
planted in 2022 due to drought conditions, a 
total of 515,000 acres of rice were planted in 
California in 2023, up 103 percent from the prior 
year. Rice production in 2023 totaled 44.0 million 
hundredweight, which represents a 99 percent 
increase from the previous year. Medium-grain 
rice represented 95 percent of the total harvested 
acreage for rice and 96 percent of total rice 
production in California. The average yield for all 
rice was estimated at 85.9 hundredweight per 
acre, down 2 percent from 2022. In 2023, the 
average yield for medium-grain rice decreased 
by 4 percent, the average yield for long-grain rice 
decreased by 5 percent, and the average yield for 
short-grain rice increased by 3 percent, compared 
to 2022.

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
Floriculture
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024     53 
In 2023, California’s floriculture crop ranked 
second in the nation behind Florida, with sales 
valued at $983 million, comprising 14.7 percent 
of the total U.S. wholesale value. California sales 
increased 2.6 percent from the 2022 value of 
$958 million. The number of California floriculture 
operations increased from 530 operations in 2022 
to 746 operations in 2023.  California had the 
most floriculture operations in the country in 2023, 
followed by Pennsylvania at 702 operations and 
Michigan at 681 operations.
For operations with more than $100,000 in sales, 
California accounted for 7.7 percent of the total 
U.S. value of bedding and garden plants. The 
value of California-grown bedding and garden 
plants was $270 million in 2023, which represents 
an increase of 6.3 percent from the 2022 value of 
$254 million.
In 2023, California’s potted flowering plants 
were valued at $249 million, which represents 
a decrease of 3.5 percent from the prior year. 
California accounted for 24.9 percent of the total 
U.S. value of potted flowering plants.
California was the leading state in cut flower 
production in 2023, accounting for 60.4 percent of 
the total U.S. cut flower wholesale value. The value 
of California’s cut flower production during the year 
was $211 million, which represents a 3.5 percent 
increase from 2022.
Floriculture

=== PDF PAGE 62 ===
Fruit and Nut Crops
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    60 
California produced 17.3 million tons of fruits and 
nuts in 2023, accounting for 65.8 percent of the 
United States’ total fruit and nut crop. The total 
value of all California fruits and nuts in 2023 was 
$21.5 billion. 
In the fruit category, California accounted for all 
U.S. production of kiwifruit, nectarines, olives, 
plums, and prunes. California also accounted for 
over 90 percent of the nation’s lemons, mandarins, 
strawberries, apricots, avocados, and grapes. For 
citrus fruit, California accounted for 81.1 percent 
of the total U.S. citrus production and 83.7 percent 
of the total value of the U.S. citrus crop in 2023, 
making it the largest citrus-producing state in the 
nation. In the nut category, California accounted 
for all of the nation’s production of almonds, 
pistachios, and walnuts.
Grapes were the highest valued fruit or nut crop in 
California in 2023, totaling $6.5 billion in production 
value. Compared to the previous year, the value 
of grapes produced in the state increased by 
16.5 percent in 2023. California was the leading 
producer of grapes nationwide, accounting for 94.3 
percent of the total U.S. grape tonnage in 2023. 
Almonds were the second highest valued fruit or 
nut crop in California in 2023, totaling $3.9 billion 
in production value. Compared to the previous 
year, the value of almonds produced in the state 
increased by 9.7 percent in 2023. California 
accounted for 74.3 percent of global almond 
production in 2023.  
Notable Increases in Production: 
Cherries, Sweet ........................................93% 
Pistachios .................................................69% 
Olives........................................................41%
Prunes ......................................................38% 
Mandarins .................................................34% 
Apples.......................................................31% 
Plums........................................................30% 
Apricots.....................................................26% 
Oranges, Navel & Misc .............................14% 
Oranges, Valencia ....................................13% 
Notable Decreases in Production: 
Dates .......................................................-27%
Kiwifruit ....................................................-24%
Grapes, Table ..........................................-16%
Avocados .................................................-15%

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===
Grape Crush
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024     82
The 2023 crush totaled 3,899,631 tons, up 6.2 
percent from the 2022 crush of 3,670,861 tons. Red 
wine varieties accounted for the largest share of all 
grapes crushed, at 1,972,249 tons, up 3.0 percent 
from 2022. White wine varieties crushed totaled 
1,712,583 tons, up 15.5 percent from the prior 
year. Raisin type varieties crushed totaled 48,910 
tons, down 54.0 percent from 2022, and table type 
varieties crushed totaled 165,889 tons, down 1.1 
percent from 2022. 
For the 2023 season, a total of 445,448 tons were 
crushed for concentrate production, accounting for 
11.4 percent of the total grape crush for the year. 
The tables in this section include grapes crushed 
for concentrate production, but do not provide 
information by district, type, or variety.
The 2023 average price of all varieties was $1,039.25 
per ton, up 11.4 percent from 2022. Average prices 
for the 2023 crop by type were as follows: red wine 
grapes, $1,345.68 per ton, up 13.5 percent from 
2022; white wine grapes, $733.54 per ton, up 6.4 
percent from 2022; raisin grapes, $288.09 per ton, 
down 6.9 percent from 2022; and table grapes, 
$194.36 per ton, down 9.2 percent from 2022.
In 2023, Chardonnay surpassed Cabernet Sauvignon 
as the variety with the largest percentage of the 
total tonnage crushed at 16.7 percent. Cabernet 
Sauvignon accounted for the second largest 
percentage of the total crush at 16.6 percent. Raisin 
grape varieties crushed for wine accounted for 1.3 
percent of the total crush, and table grape varieties 
crushed for wine accounted for 4.3 percent of the 
total crush.
District 13 (Madera, Fresno, Alpine, Mono, Inyo 
Counties; and Kings and Tulare Counties north of 
Nevada Avenue (Avenue 192)), had the largest share 
of the state’s crush at 1,040,462 tons. The average 
price in District 13 was $344.48 per ton. Grapes 
produced in District 4 (Napa County) received the 
highest average price at $7,031.87 per ton, up 5.0 
percent from 2022. District 3 (Sonoma and Marin 
Counties) received the second highest average 
price at $2,980.37 per ton, up 5.1 percent from 
2022. 
The 2023 Chardonnay average price of $1,071.43 
per ton was up 4.1 percent from 2022, and the 
Cabernet Sauvignon average price of $2,124.33 
per ton was up 8.7 percent from 2022. The 2023 
average price for French Colombard was $318.49 
per ton, down 3.1 percent from 2022, while the 
Zinfandel average price was up 15.6 percent from 
2022, at $760.91 per ton.

Grape Crush Grape Crush Report Overview
Information contained in the California Grape 
Crush Report and presented in this section was 
supplied by processors to fulfill the reporting 
requirements of Section 55601.5 of the Food and 
Agricultural Code.
The Errata to the Final Grape Crush Report 
includes all grape tonnage crushed during the 
2023 season. It also includes purchased tonnage 
and pricing information for grapes with final prices 
prior to January 10, 2024. The Errata to the Final 
Grape Crush Report, published on August 22, 
2024, contains any late reports or corrections to 
the final report. 
Details of the crushed tonnage, degrees Brix, 
and weighted average prices were reported 
by grape type and variety, as well as by grape 
pricing districts. The 17 districts refer to the area 
in which the grapes were grown as defined in the 
Administrative Code.

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===
Livestock and Dairy
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    87 
California’s total livestock and livestock products 
cash receipts were $15.8 billion in 2023, down 9.1 
percent from 2022. Cattle and calves recorded 
an increase in cash receipts of 31.3 percent, 
compared to the prior year, while the other major 
livestock categories showed a decrease in cash 
receipts for the year. Dairy and dairy products 
accounted for 51.5 percent of the total livestock 
and livestock product receipts, while cattle and 
calves accounted for 30.2 percent of the state’s 
total livestock receipts for 2023.
 
In 2023, California continued to lead the nation 
in total milk production with 40.9 billion pounds, 
representing a decrease of 2.1 percent from 
the previous year. Milk production per cow was 
down 1.9 percent from 2022, with an average 
annual yield of 23,863 pounds per cow. Total 
milk cow inventory for the state was 1,720,000 
head on January 1, 2023, which represents 
no change from the inventory recorded at the 
beginning of 2022. The annual average milk 
price received by producers during 2023 was 
$19.90 per hundredweight, which was $5.00 per 
hundredweight less than the 2022 annual average 
price. 
 
In 2023, California ranked first in the U.S. in butter, 
mozzarella cheese, Hispanic cheese, nonfat 
dry milk, and condensed skim milk production. 
California produced 32.2 percent of the nation’s 
butter and 17.5 percent of the nation’s cheese 
during the year. Italian cheeses comprised 66.4 
percent of the state’s total cheese production, 
with mozzarella accounting for 92.2 percent of the 
category. Compared to the previous year, nonfat 
dry milk production decreased by 7.1 percent in 
2023, and butter production decreased by 0.8 
percent.
 
Total production of cattle and hogs was 2.39 billion 
pounds in 2023, up 2.5 percent from 2022. Total 
marketings of cattle and hogs rose by 7.2 percent 
during the year to 2.89 billion pounds. Cattle and 
calf marketings were 2.87 billion pounds, while 
hog and pig marketings accounted for 26.1 million 
pounds. The cattle inventory was 5.1 million 
head in 2023, down 1.9 percent from the 
prior year. The hog and pig inventory was 
39,000 head in 2023, down 54.1 percent 
from 2022. 
 
California poultry and egg receipts for 2023 
totaled $2.18 billion. Total egg production in 
California decreased from 3.9 billion eggs in 
2022 to 3.2 billion eggs in 2023, representing 
a drop of 18.2 percent. The annual average 
price of eggs increased from $2.69 per dozen 
in 2022 to $2.82 per dozen in 2023. Turkey 
production rose by 2.2 percent during the 
year to 197.8 million pounds in 2023. The 
liveweight equivalent price for turkey declined 
by $0.13 per pound in 2023, compared to the 
prior year. 
 
Wool production was 2.3 million pounds in 
2023, unchanged from the prior year. The 
total number of sheep and lambs shorn in 
2023 decreased by 5.1 percent, compared 
to the prior year, to 370,000 head. Average 
wool production per animal was 6.2 pounds 
in 2023, with an average value of $1.80 per 
pound. The total number of bee colonies was 
up 6.2 percent from the prior year, totaling 
324,000 hives in 2023. Honey production 
was 13.6 million pounds in 2023, up 17.4 
percent compared to 2022.

=== PDF PAGE 102 ===
Vegetables and Melons
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    100 
California remained the leading vegetable and 
melon producing state in 2023, accounting for 46.4 
percent of U.S. harvested acres, 64.3 percent of 
the nation’s utilized production, and 63.4 percent of 
U.S. total value for the 25 selected vegetable and 
melon crops. 
In 2023, California’s vegetable and melon 
production recorded a total value of $12.23 billion, 
up from $9.76 billion in 2022. Lettuce remained the 
highest valued vegetable crop in California with a 
total production value of $3.9 billion, followed by 
tomatoes at $2.3 billion.
Honeydew melons saw the largest year-over-
year increase in production of all the vegetable 
and melon crops in the state. Total honeydew 
production in 2023 was 2.4 million cwt., up 28.1 
percent from 2022. Honeydew planted acres also 
increased to 8,400 acres in 2023, which represents 
a 16.7 percent increase compared to the prior year. 
In 2023, the total value of honeydew was $79.8 
million, which represents a 9.8 percent increase 
over the previous year. 
Both planted and harvested acres for processing 
tomatoes in California increased in 2023, 
compared to 2022, rising by 16.5 percent and 13.3 
percent, respectively. Total processing tomatoes 
production in 2023 was 12.8 million tons, which 
represents an increase of 22.0 percent compared 
to the previous year.
Vegetable and melon crops that recorded the 
largest increase in production in 2023, compared to 
the prior year, were honeydew, processing onions, 
pumpkins, Romaine lettuce, and tomatoes. Crops 
that realized the largest decrease in production 
were Agaricus mushrooms, broccoli, cantaloupe, 
cauliflower, chili peppers, garlic, snap beans, sweet 
potatoes, and watermelon.
Notable Increases in Production:
Melons, Honeydew ...................................28%
Onions, Processing ..................................22%
Tomatoes ..................................................21%
Pumpkins ..................................................16%
Lettuce, Romaine .....................................15%
Notable Decreases in Production:
Snap Beans .............................................-29%
Melons, Cantaloupe.................................-19%
Mushrooms, Agaricus ..............................-18%
Peppers, Chili ..........................................-18%
Melons, Watermelon ................................-16%
Cauliflower ...............................................-15%
Potatoes, Sweet ......................................-15%
Broccoli ....................................................-13%
Garlic .......................................................-12%

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
California Agricultural Exports
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024     109 
In 2023, California’s agricultural exports totaled 
$22.4 billion in value, representing a decrease 
of 5.9 percent compared to the previous year. 
As shown in the table below, California’s 
agricultural exports have trended upward 
over the past 10 years with total export value 
increasing by 4.0 percent since 2014.
California’s top valued agricultural export 
commodity continues to be almonds, with $4.4 
billion in foreign sales in 2023. This figure 
represents a decline of 6.3 percent from the 
previous year. Pistachios ranked second in 
export value for the year at $2.7 billion, a 23.7 
percent increase from 2022. Dairy and dairy 
products and wine came in third and fourth, 
respectively, with dairy and dairy product 
exports valued at $2.6 billion and wine exports 
valued at $1.1 billion in 2023.  
The principal 57 export commodities totaled 
$18.9 billion in export value in 2023 and 
accounted for 84.5 percent of the total value of 
California’s agricultural exports. Of the top 57 
commodities exported, 17 showed an increase 
in export value of 5 percent or more, compared 
to the previous year, while 27 commodities 
experienced a decrease in export value of 5 
percent or more. 
California’s share of total U.S. agricultural 
exports for 2023 was 12.8 percent, up from 
12.2 percent in 2022. California’s top 10 export 
destinations for 2023 – Canada, the European 
Union, China and Hong Kong, Mexico, Japan, 
India, South Korea, Turkey, the United Arab 
Emirates, and Vietnam – accounted for 66.7 
percent of the state’s total 2023 export value. 
Of the top 10 export destinations, Turkey 
experienced the largest year-over-year growth 
in total export value at 23.4 percent, followed 
by the United Arab Emirates at 20.0 percent 
and Mexico at 17.7 percent.
Methodology  
Data to compute agricultural exports are 
derived from official government sources, 
published industry sources and unpublished 
information from the government and the 
industry. Export quantities and values of the 
whole U.S. and California port districts are 
from the Department of Commerce trade data 
posted on the United States International Trade 
Commission online database (http://dataweb.
usitc.gov/). Canadian import (value) data are 
from Strategis Canada’s online database 
(https://ised-isde.canada.ca/site/trade-data-
online/en). If the California export price is not 
available for a particular product, the U.S. 
average export price for that commodity is used 
in order to provide a dollar value for exports. 
Production quantities and values are from 
various National Agricultural Statistics Service, 
Economic Research Service (ERS), and 
Agricultural Marketing Service publications.  
A share of the raw data provided in these 
United States Department of Agriculture 
(USDA) service publications are released 
as a preliminary report early in the calendar 
year followed by a more accurate final report 
released at a later date.  For accuracy, The 
University of California Davis, California 
Agricultural Issues Laboratory (CAIL) export 
estimates use data from final USDA reports. 
CAIL export estimates use different methods 
from other sources, such as ERS. The ERS 
estimates are based on a state’s national share 
of production of the exported commodity. The 
ERS method assumes that, for any particular 
California Agricultural Exports


=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
California Agricultural Exports
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024    110 
commodity, a state’s share of U.S. production 
equals that state’s share of U.S. exports. Other 
sources use geographic port data, without direct 
links to where the product originated. The CAIL 
export figures correspond to commodities that 
have been produced on farms and ranches within 
California. Products originated in another state and 
exported via California ports are not included in 
these statistics. 
 
In most cases, CAIL reports exports at the 
individual commodity level. For instance, exports 
of a given fruit in fresh, canned, and dried forms 
are summed and listed under the name of the fruit, 
although the method for fresh fruit exports may 
differ from the method used for canned and dried 
fruit. The ratio of quantity exported to quantity 
produced is given on a farm weight basis. That 
means, for example, that wine is converted back 
into fresh grapes. Standard conversion factors 
published by the USDA are applied.

$1 Billion
2023 $22.41

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
Organics
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024  122 
California’s organic agricultural industry plays an 
integral role in the state’s economy. California 
continues to lead the nation in the number of 
organic farms, land in organic production, and 
organic sales. According to the United States 
Department of Agriculture’s (USDA) National 
Agricultural Statistics Service (NASS), California 
accounts for 32 percent of certified organic sales 
in the U.S. and organic sales continue to grow in 
the state1. The California Department of Food and 
Agriculture’s (CDFA) State Organic Program (SOP) 
is committed to upholding the integrity of products 
produced and sold in California as organic.
The SOP follows the USDA National Organic 
Program (NOP) guidelines and enforces the 
Organic Food Production Act of 1990 and the 
California Organic Food and Farming Act2. 
The SOP assumes the NOP’s oversight and 
enforcement authority in the state, and California is 
the only state in the nation with a NOP-authorized 
state organic program. These laws and regulations 
protect consumers, producers, handlers, 
processors, and retailers by establishing standards 
for agricultural products and foods that are labeled 
and/or sold as organic. The SOP’s priorities are to 
protect the integrity of organic products produced 
and sold in California, while expanding outreach 
and education efforts to the organic community 
and the public. The SOP works to ensure that 
the organic food supply chain adheres to state 
and federal organic laws and regulations, so that 
California can maintain and continue to build 
consumer confidence in the organic industry.
The SOP also works in collaboration with organic 
industry leaders, representatives of the California 
Organic Products Advisory Committee, producers, 
handlers, processors, USDA Accredited Certifying 
Agencies (ACA), trade associations, county 
agricultural commissioners, and other organic 
industry stakeholders to continually improve the 
program.
California organic sales have increased by 21.7 
percent from $9.7 billion in 2019 to $11.8 billion in 
20233. Of the $11.8 billion in organic gross sales 
in California during 2023, producer gross sales 
accounted for $4,619,113,863, handler gross sales 
accounted for $5,801,248,935, and processor 
gross sales accounted for $1,365,335,996. 
Conversely, California organic production site 
acreage has decreased by 13.0 percent from 
2,051,249.17 acres in 2019 to 1,784,011.01 acres 
in 2023.
This report presents the five-year trend of organic 
sales, organic operation types, and organic 
production site acreage from 2019 to 2023, and 
provides detailed information regarding organic 
commodity harvested acreage and organic 
commodity producers for 20234. Highlights from the 
data indicate that total organic sales were reported 
at the highest level in 2021 at $12,489,183,718, 
while total organic sales were reported at the 
lowest level in 2019 at $9,686,680,228. Organic 
operation types were reported at the highest levels 
in 2021 and 2022 with 3,652 producers, 1,019 
handlers, and 152 processors for 2021, and 3,582 
producers, 1,075 handlers, and 166 processors for 
2022, while organic operation types were reported 
at the lowest level in 2023 with 3,369 producers, 
1,093 handlers, and 155 processors. Organic 
production site acreage was reported at the 
highest level in 2020 at 2,173,610.60 acres, while 
organic production site acreage was reported at 
the lowest level in 2023 at 1,784,011.01 acres.
METHODOLOGY
Through the registration process with CDFA, the 
SOP captures data related to production sites 
acreage, commodity harvested acreage, and gross 
sales for use in reporting to the organic industry, 
and for conducting enforcement activities of the 
SOP. The SOP registers every person  
Organics 


=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
Organics
California Agricultural Statistics Review 2023-2024   123 
engaged in the state of California in the production 
and handling of raw organic agricultural products, 
and processors and handlers of processed 
organic meat, poultry, dairy products, and retailers 
engaged in processing or handling of products sold 
as organic. The SOP registers both operations 
certified by an ACA and operations exempt from 
certification with an ACA5.
Through the registration application process, 
operations are required to provide their total 
acreage per site/facility, which this report 
references as “production site acreage”. 
Additionally, operations are required to provide 
their harvested commodity acreage per county, 
which this report references as “commodity 
harvested acreage”. This process allows the SOP 
to collect information for both organic production 
acreage and organic harvested acreage.
Data for gross sales for 2019 through 2023 is 
reported to the SOP by California SOP registrants, 
which includes USDA Certified Organic Operations 
and operations exempt from certification for 
organic producers, handlers, and processors.
Data for commodity harvested acreage per 
county is reported to the SOP by California SOP 
registrants from January 1, 2023 to December 
31, 2023, and includes USDA Certified Organic 
producers and producers exempt from certification.
Data for commodity producers and production 
site acreage per county is reported to the SOP 
by California SOP registrants and includes USDA 
Certified Organic producers and producers exempt 
from certification.
All statewide data is collected from the following 
SOP reports: 2019-2023 Gross Sales Reports, 
2019-2023 Commodity Harvested Acreage per 
County Reports, 2019-2023 Commodity Producers 
per County Reports, and 2019-2023 Production 
Site Acreage by County Reports.
For the purposes of registration, organic products 
are reported in accordance with the following 
regulation-specified categories:
1) Citrus,
2) Fruits, excluding citrus: strawberries (fresh 
market); all other berries; pome fruit; stone fruit; 
grapes, table; grapes, wine; grapes dried, raisins; 
all other fruits,
3) Livestock or dairy: fluid milk, cow; all other dairy 
and dairy products; cattle, beef; chicken, broilers; 
chicken, layers; all other poultry/livestock and 
products,
4) Nuts: almonds; all other nut crops,
5) Vegetables: lettuce (head, leaf, spring/salad 
mixes); broccoli; carrots; spinach (fresh and 
processed); celery/celeriac; tomatoes; all other 
vegetables, and
6) Other, which includes, but is not limited 
to, apiculture, organic fallow ground, herbs, 
mushrooms, cut flowers, and nursery: propagation; 
seed crops; all other field crops (includes pasture 
and rangeland); all other not previously reported or 
listed; fallow.
The California Agricultural Statistic Review is a 
compilation of data from various sources including 
USDA NASS, USDA Economic Research Service, 
County Agricultural Commissioners, UC California 
Agricultural Issues Laboratory, and CDFA, each 
having a separate methodology. As such, there 
may be discrepancies when directly comparing 
data presented in this section to the data presented 
in another section of this report.
1 USDA NASS 2021 Certified Organic Survey.
2 Formerly the California Organic Products Act of 2003.
3 This percentage change is calculated using the following total 
organic sales figures from the tables presented below: $9,686,680,228 
for 2019 and $11,785,698,794 for 2023; “$9.7 billion in 2019 to $11.8 
billion in 2023” are rounded figures.
4 2022 organic production acreage figures are updated from previous 
reports with the most recent information made available. 
5 This report does not include differences between operations 
certified by an ACA and operations exempt from certification, as the 
percentages presented in the previous report did not yield significant 
differences.
